“That pain has made you unpredictable. A variable. I dislike variables.” He tapped his cane once on the floor. “Your roles, however, are still concluded. More conclusively than before. You have nothing left to trade. Not even obedience.”

“What do you want?” Secundus said, stepping slightly forward, placing himself between me and Severus.

Severus’s gaze flicked to him, finally according him a moment of full attention. “I want you to understand the geometry of your situation. You are in the center of the Eyrie. You are unknown to any but my sentinel and myself. You have destroyed my property. You know a secret that could unravel a network that maintains a certain… stability in Rome. A stability that, I assure you, benefits even those it watches.”

He paused, letting the geometry settle around us like iron walls.

“You have two choices. You may exit by the door, descend the mountain, and return to your life. Your wife’s secret will remain between us. Your involvement here will be forgotten. The stability will hold. You will live with your new universal, and you will find, in time, its practical advantages.”

“Or?” I asked, though I knew.

“Or you may remain here, as permanent guests. The under-stage has many rooms. Silent ones.”

The offer was clear. Live with the lie, and live. Or cling to the truth, and die.

It was no choice at all. It was the final, elegant twist of the dramatist’s knife. He was not threatening me with death. He was offering me life, but a life that was itself a prison, a performance where I must daily act the part of the unknowing husband. The local universal of my home would become a theater of the most exquisite cruelty, and I the lead actor, forever.

I looked at the burning book. The names were gone. Aurelia Sabina’s name was ash.

But it was inside me now. A festering, eternal truth.

I looked at Secundus. His face was stone, but his eyes were on the cane in Severus’s hand, assessing its weight as a weapon, the man’s lame leg as a weakness. He was calculating the odds of a violent, messy exit. They were vanishingly small, but he was calculating them nonetheless. He was grit.

I looked back at Severus. The architect of my ruin. His patience was not magnanimous. It was the patience of a spider, sure of its web.

“The playwright is impatient,” I said, quoting his map. “You have written our conclusion. But you have miscalculated one thing.”

“Oh?”

“You believe the local universal of my life is all that matters. My marriage. My home. My peace.” I took a step toward him. The cold rage had crystallized into something diamond-hard and sharp. “You have shown me a deeper universal. That all of it—the Republic, the law, the household gods, the vows between husband and wife—is just… text. Wax on wood. Subject to revision. To erasure. To fire.”

I gestured to the brazier. “You taught me that. So I am no longer a man of principles, Severus. That luxury is burned away. I am simply a man who has seen the back of the stage. And I cannot unsee it.”

His weary eyes sharpened, just a fraction. I had his full attention now. Not as a variable, but as a new element.

“So,” he said. “Which choice?”

I did not answer him. I turned to Secundus.

“We walk out the door,” I said.

Relief, then confusion, flashed in Secundus’s eyes. He saw the resolve in my face, but not the shape of it. He gave a curt nod.

Severus inclined his head, a small, satisfied motion. The spider’s web had held. The fly had chosen the sticky, living prison over the clean kill. Aesthetic. Practical.

He stepped aside, gesturing with his cane toward the open door. A gracious host.

Secundus went first, eyes wary, body tense. I followed. As I passed Severus, our eyes met.

“Give my regards to your wife,” he said softly.

I stopped. I looked at him, this man who had turned my life into his parable. I smiled. It felt like a crack in clay.

“I will,” I said. “And I will give her your ledger. Or what’s left of it. She will understand the message.”

For the first time, something flickered in the depths of his weary eyes. Not fear. Not anger. A recalculation. He had assumed my shame would silence me. That I would bury the truth to preserve the lie. He had not considered that I might carry the ashes back to her, and that together, we might build something new from them. Something unpredictable. Something *gritty*.

He had written the end of our roles. But he had not written the next act.

I walked out of the library, leaving the playwright in the smoke of his own burned text, the open door gaping behind me like a silent, unanswered question.



---

# Chapter 20

## **CHAPTER 20**

The air in the street was a physical shock. It was not fresh—it was the thick, dung-scented, smoke-hazed air of the Subura, pressing in from all sides after the cloistered stillness of Severus’s library. But it was unowned. It carried no narrative. It was simply *there*, a gritty, indifferent reality. Secundus drew a great, shuddering breath beside me, as if he’d been drowning. He looked at me, his face pale in the late afternoon light filtering down between the tenement roofs.

“We are free?” he asked, the question a hoarse whisper. It was not a statement of fact, but a plea for confirmation of a miracle.

“No,” I said, beginning to walk. My legs moved with a strange, automatic certainty. “We have changed the terms of the captivity.”

He fell into step beside me, his shoulder brushing mine in the narrow lane. “The ledger. You truly have it?”

“A copy. Incomplete. But enough.”

“Enough for what?”

I did not answer immediately. We passed a baker’s stall, the smell of coarse bread a blunt, honest thing. A woman haggled over a string of onions, her voice a familiar, grating music. This was the world Severus considered mere set-dressing. It felt more real to me now than any principle. It was the ground under my feet.

“He believed the truth was a weapon to be sheathed,” I said finally. “To be used for leverage, for control. To keep the story tidy. He believes we will hide it, to hide our shame.”

Secundus was silent for a dozen steps. “Will we?”

I stopped, turning to face him. The weariness in his eyes was a pool I could drown in. I saw the ghost of the boy he’d been, the one who believed in the clean lines of duty and honour. That boy was gone, burned away in the same fire. What was left was raw, unadorned. It was the brother I had, not the one I wished for.

“No,” I said. “We will give it to Tullia. We will lay it before her like an offering. Not as a weapon. As a… fact. The one piece of ground that is not sand.”

He absorbed this, his gaze turning inward. “He will not allow it. He said the door was open. He did not say we could carry his property into the street.”

“He considers it his property. His text. He is wrong.” I resumed walking, the rhythm of my steps a counterpoint to the chaos of my thoughts. “It is the record of a crime against our house. It belongs to the injured party. That is the law.”

Secundus made a soft, derisive sound in his throat. “The law. An hour ago, you told him you were no longer a man of principles.”

“I am not. The law is not a principle. It is a tool. A public text. We will use it. Grittyly.”

The unfamiliar word, born in that smoke-filled room, felt solid on my tongue. It was not a virtue. It was a method.

We did not speak again until we reached the door of my home. The same door I had left from this morning, a man walking into a trap he could smell but not see. Now I returned, the trap sprung and broken in my hands, my clothes smelling of ash and cynical perfume. The *ostiarius*, old Gaius, swung the door open. His eyes, milky with cataracts, seemed to look through me. He had seen masters come and go in every state of being. My return was just another entry in his long, silent ledger.

“Domina is in the tablinum,” he murmured, stepping back.

The atrium felt like a stage set. The impluvium caught a slender rectangle of dying light, the water dark and still. The masks of my ancestors watched from their niches, their painted eyes blank. I had spent a lifetime trying to earn the right to stand among them. Now I saw them for what they were: wax and wood and pigment. Preserved lies. The household gods in their shrine were silent. I did not pour a libation. I had poured enough.

Tullia was not at the desk. She stood by the far wall, her back to me, one hand resting on the top of a wooden chest. Her posture was not one of repose, but of readiness, as if she were listening to the stones themselves. She wore a plain stola, undyed wool the colour of dust. It should have looked servile. On her, it looked like armour.

“He let you go,” she said, without turning. Her voice was flat, drained of the morning’s sharp anxiety. It was the voice of someone who had spent the hours preparing for a corpse.

“After a fashion.”

She turned then. Her eyes went to me first, a swift, assessing scan that took in the ash on my hem, the hollows under my eyes. Then they moved to Secundus, who stood a pace behind me, a shadow in his brother’s wake. Her gaze softened, just for a heartbeat, at the sight of his living face. Then it hardened again, turning to flint.

“What price?”

“A lesson in philosophy,” I said. I walked to the desk, my hand going to the inner fold of my toga. The waxed tablets were there, tied with a cord. They were warm from my skin. I drew them out and placed them on the smooth walnut with a soft *click*. “And this.”

She did not move toward it. She looked at the tablets as if they were a venomous snake, coiled and sleeping. “What is it?”

“A partial copy of Severus’s ledger. Names. Dates. Sums. The mechanics of our ruin.”

Her breath left her in a slow, controlled stream. She did not ask how I got it. She did not ask why he would give it. She asked the only question that mattered. “What does it change?”

“Everything. And nothing.” I leaned on the desk, the weight of the day settling into my bones. “He believes we will bury it. To avoid the scandal. To preserve the fiction of our honour. He believes shame is a stronger chain than iron.”

Secundus spoke from the doorway, his voice rough. “Marcus says we will not bury it.”

Tullia’s eyes snapped back to mine. In their grey depths, I saw the calculation, swift and cold as a winter stream. She was not weighing morality. She was assessing terrain. “If we make it public, we are ruined. Socially, politically. Our name becomes a punchline in every bathhouse. Our daughter’s prospects turn to ash.”

“I know.”
“If we do not,” she continued, the words precise as a surgeon’s knife, “we remain his puppets. The debt is not forgiven; it is merely a leash. He owns us. Our silence is our collar.”
“I know that, too.”
She finally moved, crossing the room with a quiet, decisive stride. She stopped before the desk, but did not touch the tablets. She looked at me. “Then you are not proposing we publish it. You are proposing we use it.”
“Yes.”
“How?”
“I do not know.”
For the first time, something flickered in her face that was not calculation. It might have been respect. Or its darker cousin, recognition. “You brought it to me. Not to use. To… share the fact.”
“Yes.”
She nodded, once. A pact sealed in something deeper than vow. She reached out then, not for the tablets, but for my hand where it rested on the desk. Her fingers were cool, dry. They did not cling; they anchored. “Then we begin from here. From this one true thing.”
She let go and picked up the tablets. She untied the cord with efficient fingers, opened the first pair. Her eyes scanned the tiny, precise script. I saw her lips move slightly, forming the names. I saw the moment she found it. Her breath hitched, a tiny, almost imperceptible sound.
“Lucius Vibenius,” she read aloud. The name hung in the quiet room like a bad smell.
Secundus stiffened. “The praetor?”
“The same,” Tullia said, her voice gone thin and cold. “A ‘consultation fee’ of twenty thousand sesterces. Dated the day after he dismissed our petition for a review of the debt.” She looked up, her eyes meeting mine. “This is not just usury, Marcus. This is corruption of a magistrate. This is a capital crime.”
The air in the room seemed to crystallize. The petty shame of debt evaporated, replaced by the colder, sharper atmosphere of treason. Severus had not just ensnared us in greed; he had woven us into a crime that could end with a public noose or a private blade in the dark. The ledger was no longer just our shame. It was a danger to far more powerful men.
“He would not have given this to you if it could truly harm him,” Secundus said, voicing the dread taking root in my own gut. “He is too meticulous.”
“He did not *give* it,” I corrected, the memory of the burning brazier sharp in my mind. “I took it from the fire. It is incomplete. Perhaps the missing pages contain the proofs, the corroborating documents. What we hold is an accusation. Without proof, it is merely a story. A dangerous, libellous story.”
Tullia closed the tablets. The soft *snap* was final. “Then it is a weapon that cuts the hand that wields it. Unless.”
“Unless?” Secundus prompted.
She set the tablets down carefully, aligning them with the edge of the desk. A fastidious, calming gesture. “Unless we find the proof. Or unless we make him believe we have found it.”
The audacity of it silenced us. The tablinum was still, the only sound the distant splash of a servant drawing water in the courtyard. My wife, who had just hours ago been preparing for widow’s weeds, was now plotting a counter-ambush against the most subtle man in Rome.
“He has agents everywhere,” I said. “He will be watching.”
“He will be watching *you*,” she said. “And Secundus. He will not be watching me.”
“Tullia—”
“Do not ‘Tullia’ me.” Her voice was low, but it filled the room. “I am not a player on his stage. I am part of the scenery. A wife. Invisible. I visit other wives. I make offerings at the temples. I manage the household accounts. Who notices the comings and goings of a woman?” She allowed herself a thin, grim smile. “He thinks in terms of men and power. His blindness is our grit.”
It was a mad plan. It was a desperate plan. It was the only plan we had. It grew not from principle, but from the raw, ugly fact of our survival. It was *gritty*.
“Where do you begin?” Secundus asked, his voice full of a brother’s fear.
“With the one person in this ledger whose shame is greater than ours,” she said. She tapped a name on the wax. “Cornelia, wife of Lucius Vibenius. She is a client of mine. She comes to me for remedies for her nerves. She lives in a beautiful house on the Palatine, and she is terrified of her own shadow. A man like Vibenius does not commit treason without his wife knowing where he hides the key.”
The world had narrowed to this: my wife, a nervous woman on the Palatine, and a few lines of wax. This was the back of the stage. The pulleys and the ropes. It was ugly. It was real.
“It is dangerous,” I said, the words inadequate.
“Life is dangerous,” she replied. She gathered the tablets, holding them against her chest. “We will hide these. Not to bury them. To keep them safe. You will go about your business. You will be seen to be defeated, resigned. You will play the fly who chose the web.”
“And you?”
“I will go and comfort a friend.” She turned to leave, then paused. She looked back at me, and in that look was everything we had built and everything we had lost. It was not love as the poets sing of it. It was something tougher, more enduring. A shared fracture. “When this is over, Marcus… we will need a new shrine. New gods. Ones that understand the world as it is, not as we were told it should be.”
She left the tablinum, her footsteps fading into the interior of the house.
Secundus let out a long, shaky breath. “She is… formidable.”
“She is all that is left,” I said. I felt emptied out. A vessel waiting to be filled with a new, bitter purpose. “We must be convincing. The performance begins now.”
The performance was one of crushing normalcy. I dined. I reviewed household matters with the steward, discussing the price of olive oil with a monotone disinterest that made the man glance at me in concern. I retired to my chamber early. I lay in the dark, listening to the sounds of the house settling. The silence from Tullia’s side of the room was a vast, listening thing.
Days passed. They were identical in their blank, featureless agony. I attended the law courts, not to plead, but to be seen. I stood in the basilica, a man among men, my face a careful mask of stoic acceptance. I exchanged greetings, discussed the weather, the races. I was Marcus Valerius, a man who had faced a reversal of fortune and was bearing it with dignified Roman grace. The performance was flawless. It tasted like ashes.
I saw Severus once, at a distance, being carried in his litter through the Forum. Our eyes did not meet. He had no need to look at me. The fly was in the web, docile. The story was proceeding as written.
On the fourth day, Tullia announced she would be visiting the Temple of Vesta to make an offering for the health of a friend’s child. It was a common, womanly errand. She met my eyes as she said it, a brief, electric contact. The friend was Cornelia.
She was gone for most of the day. The house felt like a held breath. Secundus and I avoided each other, the tension between us a live wire. He practiced with his sword in the courtyard, the rhythmic *thwack* of blade on practice post a frantic, anxious heartbeat.
When Tullia returned, the sun was low. She was calm, her movements measured as she handed her palla to a slave. She came to the tablinum where I pretended to read Cicero. She sat opposite me.
“Well?” I asked, the word cracking in the quiet.
“She knows,” Tullia said, her voice barely a whisper. “She is sick with it. Vibenius has a strongbox. Not in his study. In a false compartment behind the lararium in their bedroom. He thinks it pious. She has seen him open it. She described the mechanism.” Tullia’s fingers traced a pattern on the tabletop. “She is afraid he will be caught. She is more afraid he will kill her if he suspects she has spoken.”
“Did she agree to help?”
“No. She is a rabbit. But she will not raise the alarm. I offered her a… remedy. A stronger one than I have given before. For her nerves. It will ensure she sleeps very deeply, and remembers nothing of tomorrow night.”
A cold knot tightened in my stomach. “Tomorrow night?”
“Vibenius attends a dinner with the Pontifex Maximus. He will be gone from dusk until past midnight. Cornelia will take her draught and retire early. The house will be quiet.” She leaned forward, her eyes holding mine. “The strongbox is iron-bound oak. We will need tools. And a man who knows how to use them without noise.”
Secundus. It had to be Secundus. The soldier, the breaker of gates.
“It is a huge risk,” I said. “If we are caught in the house of a praetor, at his lararium…”
“We are already caught,” she said simply. “This is the only way out of the web. We take the proof, and we use it to cut our strings. Not to expose him publicly—that would still destroy us. To force Severus to nullify the debt. A quiet transaction. The ledger for our freedom.”
It was blackmail. It was the very thing Severus would admire for its elegant pragmatism. It was also our only move.
We called Secundus in. We laid out the map Tullia had drawn from Cornelia’s terrified description. We discussed the tools: a small pry-bar, a roll of thick cloth to muffle sound, dark, close-fitting tunics. We were not plotting as citizens, or as brothers. We were plotting as criminals. The feeling was not one of thrilling transgression, but of a grim, necessary descent. We were scraping the bottom of the world, and our fingers were finding purchase in the filth there.
The next day was an eternity. I moved through my routines like a ghost. The hours were thick, syrupy. When dusk finally began to bleed into the sky, staining it the colour of a bruise, we gathered in a storage room off the garden. We changed into the dark tunics. Secundus checked the tools, his hands steady, his face the closed mask of a legionary on a night watch. Tullia handed us each a small, hard lump of bread.
“Eat. You will need the strength.”
We ate in silence. The bread was dry, tasteless. It was the last meal of men walking to the executioner’s block, or to a battle from which they might not return.
“Remember,” Tullia said, her voice the only point of warmth in the cooling dark. “The strongbox is behind the central plaque of the lararium. It slides to the left. The mechanism is stiff. Cornelia said he uses the blade of a ritual knife to trip it.”
Secundus nodded, committing it to memory.
She looked at me then. In the dim light, her face was all shadow and resolve. “Bring it home, Marcus.”
It was not a plea. It was a command.
We left not through the front door, but through a narrow service gate that opened onto an alley. The Subura was alive with night sounds: laughter from taverns, the clatter of pots, a baby’s cry. We moved through it like shadows, two more anonymous shapes in the city’s vast, teeming dark. We crossed the Forum, empty and monumental under the stars, and began the climb up the Palatine Hill. The air grew quieter, richer with the scent of cypress and private gardens.
The house of Lucius Vibenius was not a sprawling mansion, but a compact, elegant home that spoke of tasteful wealth. A high wall, topped with shards of embedded pottery, surrounded it. The slave gate at the rear was where Tullia said it would be. Secundus produced a thin piece of forged iron from his belt. He worked at the lock, his ear pressed to the wood. There was a soft, metallic *snick*. He pushed the gate open. It groaned, a low, pathetic sound that seemed to scream in the silent night.
We were in a kitchen garden. The smell of rosemary and damp earth filled my nostrils. Light from a single lamp spilled from a high window of the main house. Cornelia’s window. The draught was working.
Secundus led, a predator’s glide in his step. I followed, my heart a frantic drum against my ribs. Every crunch of gravel under my sandal was a thunderclap. We reached a servants’ entrance. This lock was simpler. Secundus had it open in moments.
The interior was dark, warm, smelling of beeswax and dried flowers. We stood in a narrow corridor, listening. Silence, deep and complete, pressed in on us. Secundus pointed forward. We moved.
The house was a puzzle of rooms in the dark. Tullia’s directions were precise: left past the *culina*, right at the small shrine to Ceres, up five steps to the family quarters. We found the bedroom door. It stood slightly ajar.
Inside, the room was large, dominated by a wide bed. In the faint starlight from the window, I could see the shape of Cornelia under a blanket, her breathing deep and regular. And there, on the far wall, was the lararium. A niche with small, delicate statues. A faint, sweet smell of yesterday’s offering hung in the air.
We went to it. Secundus ran his fingers over the central marble plaque—a scene of Aeneas carrying Anchises. He pressed at the edges. Nothing. He took the ritual knife from its place on the shelf. He inserted the thin blade into the hairline crack at the plaque’s right edge. He levered, gently. A click, stone on stone. He slid the plaque to the left. It moved with a gritty rasp.
Behind it was a dark cavity. Secundus reached in, his arm disappearing to the elbow. He pulled. An iron-bound oak box, about the size of a large codex, emerged from the wall. It was heavy; he grunted softly as he set it on the floor.
This was it. The proof. The thing that could buy our freedom or get us killed.
Secundus set to work with the pry-bar, wrapping the tip in thick cloth. He wedged it into the seam of the lid. He leaned his weight into it. The wood protested with a long, agonized groan that seemed to echo through the silent house.
From the bed, Cornelia stirred. She murmured something in her sleep.
We froze. The sound of our own blood was deafening.
She sighed, turned over, and was still again.
Secundus looked at me, his eyes wide in the gloom. He applied pressure again, more carefully. There was a splintering crack, loud as a bone breaking. The lock mechanism gave way. The lid was open.
Inside, nestled on a bed of linen, were scrolls. Wax tablets. A few small, heavy bags that clinked with the sound of coin. Secundus reached for a scroll, but I shook my head. We did not have time to read. We had to take it all.
He pulled the linen up, making a bundle of the strongbox’s contents. He tied it swiftly. He handed it to me. The weight of it was immense. It was the weight of a man’s crime, of our future, of my family’s life.
He replaced the empty strongbox in the cavity, slid the plaque back. It would not close fully; the broken lock saw to that. But it would have to do.
We turned to leave.
And saw the figure standing in the doorway.
It was not Cornelia. It was not a house slave.
It was a man. Tall, lean, his face in shadow. He held no weapon. He simply stood, blocking our exit, a silent, waiting shape.
My blood turned to ice water. We had walked into a trap. Not Severus’s. A different one.
The man took a single step into the room, and a sliver of starlight caught his face.
It was the quiet, efficient man from Severus’s library. The one who had brought the brazier. The one who made problems disappear.
He smiled, a thin, professional curve of the lips.
“The master thought you might try something like this,” he said, his voice a soft, pleasant murmur. “He sends his compliments on your initiative. And he requests that you bring what you have found to him. Now.”



---

# Chapter 21

## **CHAPTER 21**

The weight in my arms was no longer a prize. It was an anchor. It was the proof of our transgression, bundled in stolen linen, and it pulled me toward the floor as if the stones themselves had grown hungry. The man in the doorway did not move. He was a study in negative space, a cut-out of the dim hall behind him, his stillness more threatening than any brandished blade. He had the patience of a spider at the centre of a web he had not spun, but had inherited. He was Severus’s instrument, and he was impeccably tuned.

Secundus shifted beside me, a minute tightening of his shoulders. I could feel the calculation radiating from him like heat. The distance to the door. The man’s unarmed hands. The sleeping woman in the bed, a volatile witness. The odds were not impossible. They were merely suicidal.

The man’s smile did not waver. He had read Secundus’s tension in the air. “The master is waiting,” he repeated, the words a soft, inarguable fact. “He is at the *domus* on the Caelian. He anticipated you would prefer a private conversation to a public… complication.” His gaze drifted to the bundle in my arms, then to the slightly-ajar plaque on the wall. “A neat piece of work. The lock was always a weakness. I advised a bronze casing.”

He stepped back, clearing the doorway. It was not an invitation. It was a directive. The hallway behind him was a tunnel of deeper shadow.

There was no choice. To fight was to die here, in this stranger’s bedroom, and have our bodies become just another of Severus’s administrative problems. To comply was to walk into the wolf’s den carrying the lamb ourselves. But it was a walk, not a corpse-cart. A walk held time, and time held the ghost of a chance.

I adjusted the weight of the bundle. “We seem to have lost our way,” I said, my voice a dry rustle. “Your master is a gracious guide.”

The man gave a slight, approving nod, as if I had passed a minor test. He turned and led the way out of the bedchamber. We followed, our footsteps hollow on the mosaic floor. The house was a tomb around us, every breath an echo. We passed the *lararium* where I had offered my false prayers that morning. The bronze *lares* watched us go, their faces blank with divine indifference.

He led us not to the main entrance, but to a postern gate at the rear of the property, wedged between a dripping water-cistern and a stack of broken amphorae. A covered litter waited there, its curtains drawn, the two bearers standing like statues in the pre-dawn chill. This, too, had been anticipated. The efficiency was chilling.

“Inside,” the man said, holding the curtain aside.

We climbed in. The interior smelled of stale perfume and old sweat. The curtains fell, plunging us into a darkness that was absolute. The litter lurched as the bearers took the weight, and then we were moving, the sway a nauseating rhythm. I clutched the bundle to my chest. Secundus was a solid, silent presence beside me.

“He knew,” Secundus breathed, the words so low they were almost lost in the creak of the poles. “He knew we would go there. He wanted us to find it.”

“He wanted us to bring it to him,” I corrected. The truth was a cold stone in my gut. Severus had not hidden his crime. He had archived it. And he had used Cornelia, her bitterness, her predictable rage, as the lure. We were not thieves. We were deliverymen. The humiliation of it was a taste of ashes.

“Why? To kill us with it in our hands?”

“If he wanted us dead, we would be floating in the Tiber by now,” I said. The litter turned a sharp corner, throwing us against the side. “This is a transaction. We have something he wants. He has something we want.”

“Our lives.”

“More than that,” I murmured. The bundle contained scrolls, tablets, coin. Evidence. But evidence was a currency that required a court, a judge, an audience. Severus had no need of such theatrics unless he intended to use them. He was bringing us to him. He was creating a tableau. The pieces arranged themselves in the dark, forming a vile picture. “He is not cleaning a mess. He is making a move.”

The journey was not long. The Caelian Hill was the domain of the newly, vulgarly rich, those who built high walls to keep the stench of their money from offending the old patrician noses on the Palatine. The litter stopped. The curtain was drawn back. We were in a narrow service alley, high walls on either side. The man gestured us out.

The door we entered was plain, reinforced oak. It opened into a stark, whitewashed corridor that spoke of business, not leisure. No murals, no mosaics. The floor was simple terracotta tile. This was not the public face of Severus’s domain; this was its viscera. We were led to a small, windowless room. A lamp burned on a plain table. Two stools.

“Wait,” the man said, and closed the door. The sound of a bolt sliding home was soft, final.

Secundus immediately went to the door, tested it. It did not yield. He examined the walls, the ceiling. There was no other exit. The room was a box.

“We are in the strongroom of his life,” I said, setting the bundle on the table. The clink of coin was obscenely loud. “Where he keeps the things he cannot show.”

We did not sit. We stood in the lamplight, listening to the silence. It was the silence of a well-run household in the dead hours before dawn. No slaves scurried. No voices murmured. It was the silence of a man who commanded quiet.

A long time passed. The lamp flame guttered. I studied the bundle, the neat, brutal knot Secundus had tied. Inside was the answer to who had killed the legate, and why. Inside was the reason my family was a target. And now it was back in the hands of the man who had commissioned the crime. The irony was a sharp, internal bleed.

The bolt slid back.

The door opened. Lucius Severus stood there.

He was dressed in a simple, dark *tunica*, no jewellery. His hair was neat, his face freshly shaved. He looked like a man who had not slept, but had instead spent the night in careful preparation. He carried no weapon. Behind him, in the shadow of the corridor, stood the quiet man, a silent adjunct.

Severus stepped into the room and closed the door, leaving his man outside. He looked at the bundle on the table. He looked at us. His expression was not one of anger, or triumph. It was one of profound, weary assessment.

“Marcus,” he said. “Secundus.” He walked to the table but did not touch the linen. “You have been busy. And remarkably predictable.”

“You left a trail of breadcrumbs,” I said. “We are merely hungry birds.”

A faint smile touched his lips. “Breadcrumbs. A quaint metaphor. I left a loaded cart in a ditch and waited to see who would try to steal the oxen.” He finally reached out, his fingers brushing the coarse linen. “You understand, this changes nothing. The legate is still dead. Your family is still in danger. The… instability… you have caused merely accelerates the timetable.”

“The timetable for what?” Secundus’s voice was a growl.

Severus ignored him, his eyes on me. “You sought evidence. Here it is.” He untied the knot with deliberate, precise movements. He folded back the linen. The scrolls, the tablets, the three small leather bags of coin were revealed. He picked up one bag, hefted it, and poured a stream of silver *denarii* onto the table. They rang like dull bells. “Payment. For a job done.” He selected a wax tablet, opened it, scanned the stylus marks. “Instructions. Routes, times, the legate’s habits. Quite thorough.” He dropped the tablet. It clattered onto the coins. “And this.” He lifted a scroll, its seal already broken. He unrolled a section. “A private letter from the legate to a certain tribune, expressing… reservations… about the management of the grain supply from Egypt. About certain contracts. About me.”

He let the scroll roll shut. “So. You have it all. The motive. The means. The proof of the act itself.” He spread his hands. “And what will you do with it? Run to the Praetorians? They are in my pocket. To the Senate? I own a quorum of votes. To the Emperor’s freedmen? I am a valued client.” He leaned forward, the lamplight carving deep grooves beside his mouth. “Evidence is only power if you have a platform from which to wield it. You have a doorstep and a name that smells of decay.”

The truth of it was a physical blow. He had let us steal it because it was worthless in our hands. It was only dangerous to him if it was in the hands of someone with greater power. Someone he could not control.

“Then why bring us here?” I asked. “Why not have your man bury us in Cornelia’s garden?”

“Because you are not the only hungry bird,” Severus said softly. He straightened up, his gaze turning inward. “There is a larger cart. Heavier oxen. The man who killed the legate for me… he has developed ambitions. He sees the mechanism of the thing. He understands leverage. He believes he can demand more. Not just money. A position. Influence.” Severus’s lip curled. “A rat who has tasted the master’s cheese is no longer content with the crumbs under the table. He becomes a problem.”

A cold understanding dawned. “You want us to deal with your problem.”

“I want you to deliver this,” he said, gesturing to the evidence, “to the one man in Rome who can use it against me, and who would delight in doing so. My chief rival in the grain consortium, Gaius Calvus.”

The air left the room. Secundus stared, disbelief hardening into contempt. “You want us to be your couriers? To hand your rival the rope to hang you?”

“No,” Severus said, his voice dropping to a conspiratorial murmur. “I want you to hand my rival the rope, watch him put it around my neck, and then, at the very last moment, I will hand him the stool to kick away from beneath his own feet.” He tapped the scroll. “The legate’s letter mentions Calvus as well. Not by name, but by implication. A few alterations to the text, a skilled forger’s touch… and it becomes a letter expressing the legate’s deep suspicion of Calvus’s dealings. Why was the legate killed? To silence him before he could expose Calvus’s fraud. Who killed him? The mercenary in Calvus’s pay. The evidence you so bravely uncovered and brought to Calvus—out of a desperate, noble attempt to see justice done—will, in fact, condemn him.”

The audacity of it was breathtaking. It was a play within a play, a crime folded inside its own solution. We would be his unwitting actors, delivering the prop that would destroy his enemy. And in doing so, we would publicly exonerate him. The man who had been under suspicion would be the victim of a rival’s slander. He would emerge cleaner, stronger.

“And the killer? Your ambitious rat?” I asked.

“Once Calvus is implicated, the rat will be found. A tragic suicide, full of remorse for being duped by a malicious patron. A neat end to both problems.” Severus looked at me, his eyes flat and calculating. “Your family’s safety, Marcus, for this one service. A full pardon, in effect. Your debt to me, and my debt to you for this… inconvenience… will be wiped clean. You walk away. I get what I want. Calvus falls. Order is restored.”

It was a devil’s bargain. It asked us to become accomplices in a deeper, more intricate fraud. To soil our hands not with theft, but with a perversion of justice. To save our own skins by framing an innocent man—for Calvus, though surely no saint, was innocent of this specific blood.

“And if we refuse?” Secundus said.

Severus’s affability vanished. It was like a mask slipping, revealing the weathered, ruthless stone beneath. “Then you will be arrested before noon for the murder of the legate. The evidence will be found in your possession. The very evidence on this table. Your midnight raid on the home of a grieving widow will be described in salacious detail. Your motive? The legate was about to expose your own, smaller frauds. You killed him to silence him. Cornelia will testify, a heartbroken woman who discovered your plot. My man here will testify to your confession. It will be a swift trial. A quicker execution. Your son will inherit nothing but infamy.” He paused, letting the horror settle. “Refusal is not an option. It is a death sentence for your entire line. This,” he said, nodding to the plan, “is the lifeboat. The only one I am offering.”

The room seemed to shrink. The walls pressed in. The lamp smoke coiled like a noose. I looked at the scattered coins, the scrolls, the tablets. They were no longer evidence. They were tools. And we were to be the hands that wielded them.

Severus saw the capitulation in my face before I voiced it. He nodded, the mask of the reasonable man returning. “Good. Calvus is at his villa on the Via Appia. He rarely comes to the city these days. He is paranoid, surrounded by guards. You will need a reason to see him. You will tell him you are fleeing my persecution, that you stole this evidence to trade for his protection. He will be suspicious. He will test you. You must be convincing.” He produced a small, sealed letter from his belt. “This is an introduction from a mutual acquaintance—a man who owes me a favour and does not know it. It will get you past his gate.”

I took the letter. The wax seal was plain, unmarked.

“You go at first light,” Severus said. “My man will see you to the city gate with a mule. You look the part of desperate fugitives well enough already.” He began to re-wrap the evidence in the linen, his movements efficient. He left the coins on the table. “Take these. For expenses. A show of good faith.” He pushed the two remaining bags toward me.

I did not touch them.

Secundus spoke, his voice hollow. “And after? When Calvus is ruined?”

“You return to Rome. You keep your heads down. In a month, perhaps two, you will hear that I have generously dropped all claims against you. A recognition of your past service, misplaced though it was. You will be free.” He finished tying the bundle and pushed it across the table to me. “We understand each other.”

We did. We understood perfectly. We were being inserted into the machinery of his ambition. Whether we survived the gears was of no consequence to him. We were expendable parts. If we succeeded, he won. If we failed and were killed by Calvus, we were rogue elements removed. Our only path to survival was to perform this dance flawlessly.

“We understand,” I said, the words ash in my mouth.

Severus smiled. It did not reach his eyes. “I knew you were a practical man, Marcus.” He turned and knocked once on the door. It opened. His quiet man stood there. “See them to the Appian Gate. Provide them with a mule and a waterskin. They are undertaking a journey on my behalf.”

The man nodded.

Severus left without another glance. The door remained open.

We stood in the lamplight, the bundled evidence at my feet, the blood money on the table. Secundus looked at me, his face a landscape of fury and defeat. He swept the bags of coin into a pouch at his belt. “Expenses,” he muttered, the word a curse.

The quiet man gestured. We followed him back through the sterile corridors, out through a different service entrance, into a grey, pre-dawn courtyard. A tired-looking mule was tethered there. A waterskin hung from the saddle. The sky was the colour of a bruise, the black fading to a sickly purple.

The man handed me the mule’s lead. “The Via Appia is straight. His villa is the seventh milestone. Look for the cypress avenue. Do not deviate.” He looked at us, his professional calm unbroken. “The master’s reach is long. Do not consider running. Your son is being watched as we speak.”

He had saved the final, precise turn of the knife for last. He gave a slight bow and retreated inside, closing the door.

The bolt slid home.

We were alone in the creeping dawn, with a mule, a bundle of lies, and a destination that was either our salvation or our execution ground.

Secundus let out a long, shuddering breath that misted in the cold air. “We are walking into his trap. A different shape, but a trap all the same.”

“We have always been in his trap,” I said, hoisting the bundle and tying it to the mule’s saddle. The weight was now a different kind of burden. “Now we are just being moved to a different part of the cage.”

We led the mule out of the courtyard into the empty street. Rome was at its quietest, the pause between the all-night revels of the reckless and the morning bustle of the desperate. The air smelled of damp stone and cold ashes. We walked toward the city gate, our footsteps the only sound. The bundle swayed with the mule’s plodding gait. Inside, the forged letter waited, a seed of destruction that we were to plant in another man’s house.

The plan was elegant. It was also brittle. It relied on Calvus’s greed, on his hatred for Severus, on his willingness to believe two desperate, compromised men. It relied on a forger’s skill holding under scrutiny. It relied on us playing our parts perfectly.

And it relied on Severus keeping his word.

As the massive arch of the Appian Gate loomed ahead, the first sliver of true sun cracked the horizon, bleeding red light across the rooftops. It lit the road ahead, a pale ribbon stretching into the countryside. The tomb-lined Via Appia, the road of the dead.

We passed under the shadow of the gate. The guards, bleary-eyed, waved us through without interest. Two more shabby men leaving the city, their worldly goods on a tired mule. A common sight.

We walked onto the sacred way. The tombs rose on either side, silent and watchful. The rising sun cast long, skeletal shadows from the cypress trees. The bundle of scrolls tapped rhythmically against the saddle.

Secundus walked beside me, his jaw set. After a long silence, he spoke, his voice barely audible over the crunch of gravel underfoot.

“We do not deliver it.”

I looked at him. The red dawn light was in his eyes, making them look like banked coals.

“We read it,” he said. “All of it. We learn everything. Then we decide what to deliver, and to whom.”

He was right. Severus had given us the weapon. He believed our hands were bound, that we would aim it only where he directed. But the bundle was in our possession. The journey was ours. The hours on the road were ours.

We had the evidence. Not just against Severus, but against Calvus, against the killer, against the whole rotten edifice. We had the names, the sums, the methods. Knowledge was a different kind of currency. It could be spent in more than one market.

Ahead, the road was empty, stretching toward the seventh milestone. Toward the cypress avenue and the paranoid Gaius Calvus. But between here and there were miles of solitude. Miles of opportunity.

I adjusted my grip on the mule’s lead. The weight on its back seemed to shift, no longer an anchor, but a potential. A deadly, volatile potential.

“We read it,” I agreed, the words a vow to the silent tombs. “We read every word.”

And then, perhaps, we would not be deliverymen at all.

We would be something else entirely.



---

# Chapter 22

## **CHAPTER 22**

The seventh milestone was a squat, weathered pillar of travertine, half-eaten by lichen and the teeth of time. We passed it as the sun climbed, its heat a physical weight on the wool of our cloaks. The dust of the Via Appia coated our sandals, our ankles, the mule’s fetlocks, a fine, pale powder that tasted of old bones and crushed brick. The tombs here were grander, the estates of the dead vying for immortality with facades of marble and verse carved deep into stone. *Stay, traveler, and read.* We did not stay.

Secundus walked with a new tension, a coiled vigilance that made the muscles in his jaw pulse. His eyes were no longer on the road ahead, but on the verges, the stands of cypress, the broken pediments of forgotten monuments. The bundle of scrolls was no longer a passive burden on the mule. It was a presence. It walked with us, a silent third, its secrets humming like a trapped wasp in a sealed jar.

“There,” Secundus said, his voice a low scrape. He nodded toward a side track, little more than a cart-rut veering off between two vast, crumbling *columbaria*, their facades pocked with the niches of a thousand urns. “We need walls. A roof.”

The track led to a small, derelict *ustrinum*—a private cremation ground for some long-extinct family. The main altar was a cracked slab of blackened stone. A rusted iron brazier lay on its side, a nest for weeds. But at the rear was a caretaker’s hut, its roof partially collapsed, its door a rotten plank hanging from one leather hinge.

It was perfect. A tomb among tombs.

We led the mule into the husk of the hut. Light fell in dusty shafts through holes in the terracotta tiles, illuminating motes that swirled like agitated spirits. The air was thick with the smell of dry rot, bird droppings, and the profound silence of abandonment. I untied the leather bundle, my fingers clumsy. The sensation was not of untying a package, but of breaking a seal. Of committing sacrilege.

We spread my cloak on the relatively clean dirt floor. Secundus stood guard at the doorway, his body a silhouette against the bright rectangle of the world outside. I knelt, the cool of the earth seeping through my tunic. I lifted the first scroll from its nest.

The papyrus was of good quality, tight-rolled and tied with a plain linen cord. No seal. Severus’s arrogance was breathtaking. He had not needed seals. The mere fact of his possession was security enough. Who would dare?

I broke the cord.

The script was a dense, bureaucratic hand, the ink a faded brown. An inventory. Not of grain or amphorae, but of men. *L. Caelius, vigiles, second cohort. Monthly retainer: 50 denarii. Duties: overlook shipments from Ostia, warehouses 3 and 7. Bonus for silence on the night of the Kalends of Aprilis: 200 denarii.* I read the sums, the names, the specific, grubby crimes of omission. It was a ledger of purchased blindness.

The next scroll was a copy of a Senatorial decree, a dry thing about water rights for a suburbium villa. But in the margins, in a different, sharper ink, were notes. Calculations. The name *Calvus* appeared, linked by a line to the name of the decree’s proposer. A bribe, quantified. A favour, priced.

Scroll by scroll, the edifice revealed itself. It was not a single damning letter, but a mosaic. Each tile was small, mundane: a manipulated bid for a public contract here, a buried report about a building’s unsound foundations there, a transfer of funds through a straw-man merchant in Puteoli. And woven through it all, the two threads: *Severus* and *Calvus*. Sometimes partners, their interests aligned like twin oxen pulling the same plough. Sometimes at odds, their transactions marked with a terseness that spoke of mutual contempt and deeper fear.

I read aloud, my voice a monotone in the dusty tomb-hut. Secundus listened, his back to me, his posture absorbing each fact, each figure, each name.

Then I unrolled the final scroll. It was shorter, on a different, slightly finer papyrus. The hand was not a clerk’s. It was educated, fluid, but with a controlled ferocity in the downstrokes. It was a letter. Unsigned.

*To the man of the Esquiline: The merchandise from Alexandria is delayed. The seas are treacherous this season. Your partner on the Palatine grows restless. His investments are idle, and idle gold burns a hole in his purse. He speaks of finding new agents, of streamlining the enterprise. He questions the division of labour. More, he questions the need for a partner at all. The incident with the dancer was clumsy. It draws eyes. Your partner believes a cleaner cut is now required. He is auditing the books. All books. Ensure your own ledgers are in order. Or ensure they cannot be read.*

I let the papyrus curl back onto itself. The silence in the hut was absolute, broken only by the distant cry of a crow.

“The dancer,” Secundus said, still facing the light. “The girl from the Subura. The one who saw the body moved.”

“The incident that was clumsy,” I said. The words from the scroll hung in the air. *A cleaner cut.* “She wasn’t killed to silence her about the murder. She was killed because her killing—the method of it—drew our eyes. Calvus thought it messy. He’s tidying up.”

“And Severus is a loose thread,” Secundus finished. He turned. The shafts of light cut across his face, leaving one eye in shadow, the other a gleam of cold understanding. “The paranoid partner. Auditing the books. This,” he gestured to the scroll in my hand, “is Severus’s insurance. Not just against us, or the law. Against *him*.”

The understanding was a cold flood in my gut. We had been pawns in a game, but the game was older and more vicious than we knew. Severus wasn’t just using us to strike at Calvus. He was using us to pre-empt his own assassination. He was firing us, a last, desperate arrow from a besieged wall, hoping we would strike true before the gate was stormed.

The weight of the knowledge was immense, a pressure behind my eyes. We held not just evidence of crime, but the live fuse of a feud between two vipers. Delivering this to Calvus would be throwing oil on a smouldering fire. It would trigger the “cleaner cut” immediately. Severus would be dead by nightfall, and we would be standing in the room when the blade was drawn. The most inconvenient witnesses of all.

“We cannot go to Calvus,” I said. The conclusion was a stone, final and heavy.

“And we cannot go back to Severus,” Secundus said. “He expects the blow to have been struck. He will have men watching Calvus’s villa. If we do not emerge, or if we emerge without having delivered his package… we are failed tools. Tools to be discarded.”

We were in the narrow space between the hammer and the anvil. The road had run out.

Secundus crouched opposite me, his gaze on the scattered scrolls. “We have the currency. But the market is closed. Both buyers will kill the merchant.”

“Then we find a new market,” I said. The idea formed like a cyst, painful and inevitable. “A third party. Someone with an interest in seeing both men ruined. Someone who can wield this,” I touched the papyrus, “without being poisoned by it.”

Secundus barked a short, humourless laugh. “A senator of integrity? In Rome? Show him this, and he will use it to secure a better price for his own silence.”

“Not a senator,” I said. The local truth of our world—the grubby, intricate web of greed and fear—collided with a universal one: the sheer, exhausting weight of power. To break it, you needed a force that stood outside it. A force that was, itself, a different kind of power. “The Praetorian Prefect. Sejanus.”

Secundus went very still. The name was a spell, one that conjured images of silent power, of a shadow that stretched longer than the Emperor’s own. A man who fed on the secrets of others, who cultivated chaos in order to be the only source of order.

“He is a wolf,” Secundus said, his voice hushed. “We would be walking into the den, offering him meat.”

“He is the only wolf big enough to eat the other two,” I replied. The logic was terrifying in its clarity. “He has no love for Calvus—the man is a rival in the Emperor’s ear. And Severus is a useful tool, but a disposable one. This,” I gestured to the ledger of corruption, “is a gift for him. It gives him cause to remove both, publicly. To tighten his grip. He gets order, and credit for cleansing the state.”

“And what do we get?” Secundus asked. The question of the small man in the vast machine.

“We get to live,” I said simply. “Perhaps. If we are very, very careful. We become his witnesses. The source of the information. We trade being pawns for Severus for being… assets for Sejanus. It is not freedom. It is a different cage.”

“But a cage with a future,” Secundus murmured. He looked at the scrolls, seeing them now not as a weapon to aim, but as a ticket of entry to the most dangerous room in the world.

The decision settled over us, not with a rush, but with a slow, sinking finality, like a stone finding its bed in deep mud. There was no triumph in it. No righteous plan. Only the grim arithmetic of survival.

We re-rolled the scrolls with meticulous care, restoring them to their exact order. The bundle, retied, seemed heavier now, charged with a new and more terrible destination. We could not go to the Palatine openly. A praetor and his scribe, begging audience with the Prefect? We would be dead in a gutter before we reached the first checkpoint.

“We need an intermediary,” I said, brushing the dust from my knees. “Someone who can get a message through. Someone he might see.”

Secundus thought, his eyes slits of concentration. “The Prefect’s network is vast. But he has… collectors. Men who bring him interesting things. I know of one. A freedman named Lycus. He runs a shipping agency by the Emporium. He is a whisperer. He moves gossip and goods. If we present him with a sample,” he tapped the bundle, “he will know its value. He will know how to get it to the man who values such things.”

It was another risk. Adding a link to the chain. But chains were all we had left.

We emerged from the tomb-hut into the late afternoon sun. The world was unchanged—the same road, the same dust, the same silent, watching dead. But we were altered. Our path had bent, twisting back toward the city we had just left, toward a heart of power darker than any we had yet approached.

We walked not toward the eighth milestone and Calvus’s villa, but back the way we had come. The mule, sensing the shift, plodded with a resigned weariness. The seventh milestone passed again on our left, a mute witness to our reversal.

As we walked, Secundus laid out the plan, his voice low and steady. The Emporium district would be bustling until dusk. We would find Lycus. We would show him a single scroll—the unsigned letter. It was the most incendiary, the clearest proof of the deadly rift. It would be our hook. The rest we would keep hidden, our only remaining leverage.

The city walls grew before us, the Appian Gate now a dark maw swallowing the stream of travellers returning before curfew. The guards were more alert now, their eyes scanning faces, packs. We melted into the flow of labourers and farmers, our heads down, two more anonymous men covered in the grime of the road.

The transition from the tomb-silence of the Appia to the roaring, stinking cacophony of the city was a physical blow. The press of bodies, the shout of hawkers, the clatter of carts on stone—it was a world of violent, teeming life. It felt alien. We carried the silence of the tombs within us, a cold core the noise could not touch.

The Emporium was a labyrinth of warehouses and stalls, the air thick with the smells of spices, cured fish, hemp, and sweat. Secundus led us through the chaos with a hunter’s certainty, down a side-alley where the buildings leaned so close they blotted out the sky. A sign, faded, showed a ship’s prow and the name **LYCUS & SON**.

The ‘son’ was a fiction. Lycus was a man who traded in information, and children were a liability.

The office was a single room at the back of a warehouse stacked with Egyptian linen. A clerk, a young man with the wary eyes of a ferret, looked up from an abacus.

“We are here about a delayed shipment,” Secundus said, his tone flat, official. “From Alexandria. The seas were treacherous.”

The clerk’s eyes flickered. A code. He stood without a word and disappeared behind a heavy curtain. A moment later, he returned and nodded us through.

Lycus was a small man, perfectly groomed, seated behind a desk of citrus wood. He had the soft hands of a clerk and the eyes of a garum fisherman—they missed nothing. He did not rise.

“Alexandria is far,” he said, his voice a smooth, neutral instrument. “Many things are delayed. Or lost.”

“Some things are too valuable to lose,” I said. I stepped forward and placed the single, unrolled scroll on his desk. I did not let go of the edges. “This was nearly lost. It concerns a partnership. On the Palatine and the Esquiline. The partnership is dissolving. Acrimoniously.”

Lycus’s eyes dropped to the papyrus. He read. His face remained a polite mask, but a single muscle in his jaw tightened, once. He read it twice. Then he leaned back, steepling his fingers.

“This is a private matter between two… businessmen,” he said.

“It is a matter of state,” Secundus corrected, his voice like iron. “The business is the corruption of the state. The dissolution involves murder. And the intended remedy is more murder. It is the kind of disorder that… displeases certain authorities.”

Lycus studied us. He saw a tired praetor and a hardened scribe, dust from the road of the dead still in the folds of their clothes. He saw men who had stepped out of their depth and were now treading water in a shark-filled sea. He also saw the scroll.

“You have more,” he stated.

“We have the entire account,” I said. “This is the summary. The rest is… detailed. We wish to place it in the hands of the only authority capable of restoring order. We seek an introduction.”

Lycus was silent for a long count. The sounds of the Emporium were a distant rumble. He was calculating, weighing the value of the merchandise against the danger of the sellers. We were not his usual suppliers. We were trouble.

But the merchandise was exquisite.

“Wait here,” he said finally. He stood, taking the scroll with him. He moved to a locked cabinet, produced a key, and placed the scroll inside. He relocked it. “Do not touch anything.”

He left through a rear door. The clerk brought us no wine. We stood in the stuffy office, the smell of linen and ink in our nostrils. Minutes stretched. Secundus positioned himself where he could see both the curtain to the front and the door Lycus had used. His hand rested near the hilt of his dagger.

Perhaps half an hour passed. The light in the high window began to soften toward evening.

Lycus returned. He was alone. His mask was perfectly back in place.

“You will come with me,” he said. “Now. Bring your… inventory. Leave the mule.”

“Where?” I asked.

“To a house on the Caelian. A quiet house. You will be received.”

The Caelian Hill. Not the Praetorian barracks. A private residence. It was better, and worse. More deniable.

We retrieved the full bundle from the mule’s pack. I felt a last, foolish pang of loyalty to the beast, standing dumb and tired in the alien warehouse. We left it there.

Lycus led us out the back, into a maze of service alleys. A closed, unmarked litter waited, carried by four large, silent Cappadocians. We climbed in. The curtains were drawn. The world became a swaying, darkened box, the sounds of the city muffled to a murmur.

The journey was not long. The incline told us we were climbing the hill. The litter stopped. We were ushered out into a secluded courtyard, walled on all sides. The house was large, unostentatious, its power expressed in the sheer thickness of its walls, the height of its gates, the absolute quiet.

A steward, older than time and just as expressionless, met us. Lycus exchanged a nod with him and melted away, his part done. The steward’s eyes swept over us, noting the dust, the weariness, the bundle under my arm.

“You will wait in the triclinium,” he said. His voice was dry as papyrus. “You will touch nothing. You will speak only when addressed.”

He led us through an atrium devoid of the usual busts of ancestors. The impluvium held still, black water. The house felt empty, a shell. The triclinium was a spare, formal room. Three couches arranged around a low table. A single, excellent statue of Minerva in a niche. No frescoes. No clutter.

“Wait,” the steward said, and left, closing the door behind him.

We stood. We did not sit. The bundle in my hands was now the only real thing in the world. The silence of the house was deeper than that of the tombs. It was the silence of watched, calculated space.

Time lost its meaning. The light from the high window faded to twilight grey.

Then the door opened.

He entered alone. He was not a large man, but he filled the room. He wore a simple, dark tunic, no insignia. His hair was trimmed close, his face an ageless, intelligent mask, neither kind nor cruel. It was a face that had learned to reveal nothing, so that every twitch, every glance, became a weapon. His eyes were the colour of a winter sky, and they took us in with a single, comprehensive sweep that felt like being flayed.

Tiberius’s shadow. The power behind the throne. Lucius Aelius Sejanus.

He did not greet us. He walked to the couch at the head of the room and sat, leaning back as if weary. His gaze settled on the bundle.

“So,” he said. His voice was calm, medium-pitched, utterly devoid of warmth or hostility. It was the voice of a man assessing a report. “You have brought me a problem.”



---

# Chapter 23

## **CHAPTER 23**

The silence after his words was not empty. It was a substance, thick as the black water in the impluvium, and it poured into the space between us, filling our mouths, our lungs. Sejanus did not move. He was a study in calculated stillness, a man who had mastered the art of making others bleed simply by waiting for them to flinch. The bundle under my arm—the oilskin-wrapped, cord-bound testament of a dead man’s fear—felt like it was burning through wool and flesh to brand the bone beneath.

I did not speak. I had been told not to. The command of the steward was a lesser echo of the power in this room, and I was a man built on the bedrock of obedience to such hierarchies. My throat was a dry column of dust.

It was Cassia who broke the silence. Her voice, when it came, was clear and low, a blade drawn without a sound. “We have brought you the truth, Prefect. Whether it is a problem is for you to judge.”

Sejanus’s winter-sky eyes shifted to her, a slow, deliberate transfer of weight. I felt the absence of his gaze like a chill. He examined her as he had examined the room: comprehensively, without prejudice, stripping away the travel-stained tunic, the loose braid of hair, the weary set of her shoulders. He saw the senator’s daughter beneath the dust. He saw the woman who had walked out of the Subura with a butcher’s knife. He saw, I was certain, everything.

“Cassia Virilia,” he said. Not a question. A filing. “Your father wept when you refused the match with the Claudian. He said you had the *mos maiorum* of a Vestal and the stubbornness of a Corsican mule. I see he was only half-wrong. The Vestals are pragmatic.”

He knew her. Of course he knew her. He knew the lineage of every patrician spine he had ever considered breaking. Her father’s tears were a data point in his ledger.

He returned his gaze to me. “And you are the *lictor*. The one who finds things. Who asks questions in tombs.” A faint, almost imperceptible line appeared between his brows, the ghost of a man cross-referencing a memory. “You served in Illyricum. Under Drusus. A solid soldier, your record says. Not ambitious. A man who knows his place.”

He was drawing our outlines, pinning us to the board of his understanding. Reducing lives to notations. It was a form of violence more intimate than a blow.

“The place of a citizen is to serve the State,” I said. The words were the old, hard currency of my life, worn smooth by use.

“The State,” Sejanus repeated, and the word in his mouth became something fluid, shifting. “A noble abstraction. We who serve in the shadows must deal with its more tangible components. Flesh. Ambition. Fear.” He leaned forward, just a degree, and the room leaned with him. “Show me.”

It was not a request. I stepped forward, the geometric patterns of the floor mosaic seeming to shift under my boots. I laid the bundle on the low table between the couches. The sound it made was a dull, final thud. I stepped back, aligning myself with Cassia once more. We were supplicants before an altar of a new and terrible god.

Sejanus did not reach for it immediately. He looked at it as a physician might look at a corrupted wound. Then, with a single, precise finger, he drew the bundle toward him. He untied the cords with methodical care, his movements economical, devoid of haste or curiosity. He peeled back the oilskin. The wax tablets, the scroll, the small, cloth-wrapped lump of Gallus’s signet ring were laid bare under the grey twilight of the high window.

He took the scroll first. He unrolled it, his eyes scanning the lines of Gallus’s frantic, spidery script. His expression did not change. It was the face of a man reading a grain shipment report. He set it aside. He opened the wax tablets, reading the depositions from the mine overseer, the scribbled calculations of silver diverted, the names. Names that were not just names—they were men in the Senate, men with provincial commands, men who dined at Tiberius’s table on the Palatine. He closed the tablets with a soft click.

Finally, he picked up the cloth, unfolded it. The iron ring, with its intaglio of a bristling boar, lay in his palm. He held it up to the fading light, turned it once, then let it fall back onto the cloth with a quiet, metallic tap.

The silence returned, deeper now, charged with the after-image of the words he had consumed. He leaned back again, steepling his fingers before his lips. His gaze was fixed on a point somewhere in the middle distance of the room, seeing not the wall, but the vast, intricate machine of empire, its gears and levers and hidden stresses.

“A problem,” he said again, softly, as if to himself. Then his eyes focused on us. “Gallus was a fool. A greedy, sentimental fool. He thought to buy a legacy with stolen silver. He thought the love of a daughter was a commodity to be traded alongside Iberian ore.” He spoke not with anger, but with a profound, weary contempt. “He kept records. Only a fool keeps records. But he was, in the end, a slightly cleverer fool. He knew a record is also a weapon. And he aimed it, from beyond the pyre, at me.”

“He believed you were part of the scheme,” Cassia said. Her voice was tight, a wire under tension. “That you protected the diversion.”

Sejanus almost smiled. It was a cold, minute flexing of muscles. “He believed I would notice a few talents of silver going astray from a mine in the armpit of Hispania? The State bleeds from a thousand such small cuts. My concern is the artery, not the capillary.” He gestured toward the evidence. “This is not a revelation of corruption. Corruption is the weather. It is the sea in which we swim. This,” he said, and his finger tapped once, decisively, on the scroll, “is a map. A map of loyalties. Of obligations. Of men who, when pressed, will choose their own skin over their allegiance to Tiberius. Gallus did not bring this to me to expose thieves. He brought it to me as an insurance policy. He thought to make himself useful. To make his knowledge a leash around my neck.”

He fell silent, letting us navigate the implications. The room seemed to grow colder. This was not about justice. It was not even about crime. It was about topology—the mapping of power, the identification of weak strata in the bedrock of the regime. Gallus’s shame was a mere footnote.

“And the girl?” I asked. The question left me before I could cage it. “His daughter. Aelia. Was she just another notation?”

Sejanus’s gaze settled on me, and I felt the full, unnerving weight of his attention. It was like being looked at by a statue that had decided, just for you, to animate. “The girl was a liability. A thread that could be pulled. Gallus’s enemies used her. My enemies used her. They thought to use her to make him act, to make him run, to make him do something foolish that would expose a wider network. They did not count on his sentiment. Or on his cowardice. He chose to die and leave his weapon behind, rather than fight for her.” He said it without judgement. It was a tactical assessment. “She is dead?”

“Yes,” Cassia said, the word a stone dropped into a well.

“A simplification,” Sejanus noted. He looked back at the materials. “And you. You have walked this map. You have spoken to the names. You have seen the connections. You understand what this represents.”

It was not a question. We did understand. We understood that we were no longer holding evidence of a crime. We were holding a live coal. A list of men who were compromised. Men who, if this information became public, would be ruined. And ruined men, men of rank, do not fade quietly. They lash out. They seek new patrons. They become weapons for other, larger enemies. The exposure of this “problem” would not cleanse the State; it would destabilize it. It would create a vacuum, and into vacuums rush chaos, and ambition, and blood.

The true, dark genius of Sejanus’s position revealed itself. He did not want the theft punished. He wanted the knowledge of the theft contained. He wanted to *own* the weakness of these men. Their guilt was not a scandal to be aired; it was a lever to be kept, oiled and ready, in his hand. To act on this information was to break the levers. To bury it was to keep them.

We had not brought him a problem. We had brought him a treasury of influence.

And in doing so, we had made ourselves part of the ledger.

“The man who killed the girl,” I said. “The one who hunted us. He was not one of the thieves.”

“No,” Sejanus said. “He was a cleaner. Employed by someone who saw Gallus’s stumbles as an opportunity to… tidy up. To sever loose threads before they could be noticed by higher authority.” He meant himself. The cleaner had been employed by a rival within the shadow-world, someone trying to manage a crisis without Sejanus’s knowledge. A small, brutal war in the darkness. “He has been dealt with.”

*Dealt with*. The finality of it was absolute. Lycus, melting away. The steward’s dry voice. The empty, watchful house. The cleaner was now a ghost in a ditch somewhere, another silent entry in Sejanus’s ledger.

“And us?” Cassia asked. She lifted her chin. “Are we loose threads?”

Sejanus considered her. For the first time, I saw a flicker of something that might have been genuine, if clinical, interest. “You are a complication. The *lictor* is a known quantity. A tool. But you… you left your place. You walked into the dark and you retrieved the weapon your father was too cowardly to wield. You are an anomaly.” He paused. “Anomalies can be dangerous. They can also be useful.”

He rose then, smoothly. The audience was over. He gathered the oilskin and its contents into a single, neat package. He held it not like a secret, but like a standard piece of administrative apparatus. “You will remain here tonight. You will speak of this to no one. Not to each other. Not to the walls. In the morning, you will be given instructions.”

He walked to the door. With his hand on the latch, he turned back. His ageless face was once more a mask, but his eyes held a final, chilling clarity. “You sought to serve the State. You have. The State, as it currently stands, requires silence. Your service now is to become a part of that silence. It is the highest duty you will ever perform.”

He left. The door closed without a sound.

The steward returned moments later, his footsteps whispering on the mosaic. He led us not back through the atrium, but down a narrow, plain corridor to a small, cell-like room. It contained two simple cots, a jug of water, a bowl, a chamber pot. A single, high, barred window showed a square of black night sky. He placed a loaf of bread and a wedge of cheese on a small stool.

“You will stay,” he said, echoing his master’s words. “You will wait.”

He left, and we heard the distinct, heavy sound of a bolt sliding home on the outside of the door.

We were not guests. We were not prisoners. We were assets in storage.

Cassia stood in the centre of the room, her arms wrapped around herself. The steel that had carried her from the Subura, through the tombs, to the heart of the wolf’s den, seemed to have left her. She looked young, and hollowed out by a terrible understanding.

“It was all for nothing,” she said, her voice barely a breath. “Aelia. Gallus. All of it. It just… goes into his box. To be used. We didn’t bring justice. We brought him a better tool.”

I went to the water jug, poured some into the bowl. The action was automatic, a soldier’s habit. I handed it to her. She took it but did not drink.

“We are the silence,” I said. The words tasted of ash.

I sat on the edge of a cot. The straw stuffing crackled. I felt the weight of every mile from Rome, every moment of fear, every spark of futile hope, settle into my marrow. I had done my duty. I had followed the trail. I had delivered the truth to the highest authority I could conceive. And the authority had thanked me by showing me that the truth was not a light to be shone, but a resource to be hoarded. The *mos maiorum*, the sacred way of the ancestors, the unbroken chain of duty and honour—it ended here, in this quiet room, with a bolted door and a man who saw the world as a system of pressures and controls.

Cassia sat on the other cot, facing me. She set the bowl down. “What will he do with us?”

I had seen the fate of tools that had served their purpose. Some were kept oiled in the shed. Most were discarded when they became too worn, or when a better tool was found. “He will assess our usefulness. My record is clean. I am a lictor. I can return to my place, a quieter man, a man who has seen how the world truly works and will ask no more questions. I am… sustainable.”

“And I am an anomaly,” she finished. Her eyes were dark pools in the faint light. “An unmarried woman of rank who has seen the machinery. I cannot be put back. I am a loose thread in the weave.”

“He said anomalies can be useful.”

“Useful how?” A flash of her old fire returned, bitter now. “As a wife to some client of his? A silent ornament in some distant province? Or a body in a ditch, like the cleaner?”

We lapsed into silence. The night deepened outside the high window. We ate the bread and cheese without tasting it. We drank the water. We did not speak of the tomb, or the chase, or the dead girl. Those things belonged to the story we had thought we were in. A story of discovery and consequence. That story was over. We were in a new story now, one written in a language of cold utility.

I lay on my cot, staring at the ceiling beams. I thought of the *lararium* in my home, the bronze gods blurred by smoke. I had made libations for the dead, for the memory of a duty I thought I understood. I had believed in a world where actions had moral weight, where the scales, though slow to tip, eventually found their balance. It was a child’s world. The real world, the world of Lucius Aelius Sejanus, had no scales. It had a balance sheet. It had levers. My devotion, my prostration on the cold marble, was just another form of energy to be harnessed or discarded.

Cassia’s breathing evened out into the shallow rhythm of exhausted sleep. I did not sleep. I listened to the silence of the house. It was not the silence of peace. It was the active, humming silence of a mind at work. The silence of calculation.

Just before dawn, when the square of sky was fading from black to iron grey, I heard it. A sound so soft it was almost a thought. A scrape. Then a faint, metallic *snick*.

Not at the door. At the window.

I lay perfectly still, my eyes open to slits. The high, barred window was a deeper grey against the wall. A shadow passed across it, blotting out the light for an instant. Then a hand appeared, gripping the bars from the outside. A lean, strong hand. There was a faint grinding sound, a squeak of protest from aged mortar. Then one bar, then another, came free, lifted silently out from their sockets. They had been prepared. Weakened.

A figure dropped into the room, landing in a crouch on the floor with the liquid grace of a cat. He straightened. He was dressed in dark, close-fitting clothes, a hood shadowing his face. In the gloom, I could see the glint of a blade in his hand. Not a dagger. A short, efficient sword.

He looked first at Cassia, asleep on her cot. He took a silent step toward her.

My body moved before my mind could form a command. I rolled from the cot, coming to my feet between him and her. The cot frame groaned. Cassia awoke with a start, a gasp caught in her throat.

The intruder froze. He had not expected me to be awake. His head tilted, the hood shifting. He was assessing, recalibrating. This was not a frenzied attack. It was a delivery.

“Who sent you?” I breathed, my voice a dry rasp. “Sejanus?”

A low, humourless laugh came from the hood. “Sejanus has you where he wants you. I am from the other side of the ledger.”

The rival. The one who had employed the cleaner. The one trying to manage the crisis. Sejanus had dealt with the cleaner, but the master remained. And the master could not allow two people who had walked the map, who had held the evidence, to live in the custody of his enemy. We were a vulnerability. Loose threads.

“He will know it was you,” Cassia said, rising to stand beside me. Her voice was steady. Cold.

“He will suspect. Suspicion is the currency of our trade. But a dead anomaly and a dead lictor, slain in the night by person or persons unknown… that is a closed account. He may be angry, but he will not waste resources avenging closed accounts.”

He took another step. The sword point lifted, aimed at the centre of my chest. He was professional. He would kill me first, the greater physical threat, then her. It would be quick.

My hand went to my belt, where my own knife should have been. It was gone, of course. Taken by the steward. I was unarmed. I had only my body, forty-seven years old, with its symphony of complaints. I had the training of Illyricum, decades old. I had the memory of Drusus’s voice shouting drills on a muddy field.

And I had the weight of the silence I had just promised to become.

The intruder lunged. It was not a dramatic thrust, but a sharp, efficient extension of his arm, aiming to pierce my heart and be done.

I did not try to dodge. I stepped *into* it, turning my body at the last instant. The blade seared along my ribs, tearing wool and skin.
